**Starlink** **Services, LLC** is a [telecommunications](33094374.md) subsidiary of the American spaceflight company [SpaceX](832774.md),[^cite_note-2] providing broadband Internet service in approximately 160 countries and territories.[^cite_note-Bhowmik_2026-3][^cite_note-ForbesApr2025-4] It also aims to provide global [mobile broadband](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_broadband).[^cite_note-cnbc-20220825-5] SpaceX began launching Starlink [satellites](27683.md) in 2019. As of June 2026, the network consisted of approximately 10,413 satellites (10,397 operational)[^cite_note-6] in [low Earth orbit](47568.md) (LEO) that communicate with user terminals and gateway [ground stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ground_station) connecting the constellation to terrestrial [internet infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_infrastructure). Starlink also provides [in-flight internet access](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passenger_Wi-Fi_on_airplanes) for numerous airlines. Starlink accounts for approximately 75% of all active maneuverable satellites in Earth orbit and had more than 12 million subscribers as of June 2026.[^cite_note-S-1-1][^cite_note-Bhowmik_2026-3]

In May 2018, SpaceX estimated the cost of designing, building, and deploying the constellation to be at least US\$10 billion.[^cite_note-nsf20180517-7] By the end of 2025, Starlink had become SpaceX's largest business segment, generating \$11.4 billion in [revenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revenue) and \$4.4 billion in [operating income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operating_income).[^cite_note-S-1-1]

In addition to civilian broadband service, Starlink is also used for government and military communications, including notably [in the Russo-Ukrainian war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink_in_the_Russo-Ukrainian_war).[^cite_note-8] Its large satellite constellation has been criticized by astronomers for increasing light pollution and orbital congestion.[^cite_note-9][^cite_note-10]

## History

### Background

Constellations of [low Earth orbit](47568.md) (LEO) satellites were first conceptualized in the mid-1980s as part of the United States [Strategic Defense Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Defense_Initiative), culminating in [Brilliant Pebbles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brilliant_Pebbles), where weapons were to be staged in low orbits to intercept [ballistic missiles](38025.md) on short notice. The potential for low-[latency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latency_\(engineering\)) communication was also recognized, and development offshoots in the 1990s led to numerous commercial LEO systems using fewer than 100 satellites, such as [Celestri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celestri), [Iridium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iridium_satellite_constellation), and [Globalstar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalstar). [Teledesic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teledesic) proposed a broadband "Internet in the Sky," similar to Starlink, with 840 satellites. However, some of those entities, including Iridium and Globalstar, went bankrupt. Teledesic voluntarily wound down operations due to financing challenges in the wake of the [dot-com bubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dot-com_bubble) burst.[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12]



Musk and Larry Williams meet with [Michael D. Griffin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_D._Griffin), long-time proponent of low Earth orbit constellations for military purposes (2005).

In 2004, Larry Williams, SpaceX VP of Strategic Relations and former VP of [Teledesic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teledesic), opened the SpaceX Washington DC office.[^cite_note-13] That June, [SpaceX](832774.md) acquired a 10% stake in [Surrey Satellite Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrey_Satellite_Technology) (SSTL) as part of a "shared strategic vision".[^cite_note-14] SSTL was at that time working to extend the Internet into space.[^cite_note-15] However, SpaceX's stake was sold back to [EADS Astrium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EADS_Astrium) in 2008 after SSTL became more focused on navigation and Earth observation.[^cite_note-16]

In early 2014, [Elon Musk](909036.md) and [Greg Wyler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greg_Wyler) worked together planning a constellation of around 700 satellites called [WorldVu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WorldVu_Satellites_Ltd), which would be over 10 times the size of the then-largest constellation, [Iridium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iridium_satellite_constellation).[^cite_note-17] However, these discussions broke down in June 2014, and SpaceX instead filed an [International Telecommunication Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Telecommunication_Union) (ITU) application via the [Norwegian Communications Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_Communications_Authority) under the name STEAM.[^cite_note-18] SpaceX confirmed the connection in the 2016 application to license Starlink with the [Federal Communications Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Communications_Commission) (FCC).[^cite_note-19] SpaceX trademarked the name *Starlink* in the United States for their satellite broadband network;[^cite_note-20] the name was inspired by the 2012 novel *[The Fault in Our Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fault_in_Our_Stars)*.[^cite_note-21]

### &#x20;Design phase (2015–2016)

Starlink was publicly announced in January 2015 with the opening of the SpaceX satellite development facility in [Redmond, Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redmond,_Washington). During the opening, Musk stated there is still significant unmet demand worldwide for low-cost broadband capabilities,[^cite_note-sn20161010shotwell-22][^cite_note-FCC20140623-23] and that Starlink would target [bandwidth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandwidth_\(computing\)) to carry up to 50% of all [backhaul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Backhaul_\(telecommunications\)) communications traffic, and up to 10% of local Internet traffic, in high-density cities.[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-announcementEvent-25] Musk further stated that the positive cash flow from selling [satellite internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_internet) services would be necessary to fund SpaceX's [Mars plans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Mars_program).[^cite_note-sn20180312-26] Furthermore, SpaceX has long-term plans to develop and deploy a version of the satellite communication system to serve [Mars](14640471.md).[^cite_note-27]

Starting with 60 engineers, the company operated in 2,800 m<sup>2</sup> (30,000 sq ft) of leased space, and by January 2017 had taken on a 2,800 m<sup>2</sup> (30,000 sq ft) second facility, also in Redmond.[^cite_note-28] In August 2018, SpaceX consolidated all their Seattle-area operations with a move to a larger three-building facility at Redmond Ridge Corporate Center to support satellite manufacturing in addition to [research and development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_and_development).[^cite_note-gw20181031-29] In July 2016, SpaceX acquired an additional 740 m<sup>2</sup> (8,000 sq ft) creative space in [Irvine, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irvine,_California).[^cite_note-30] The Irvine office would include [signal processing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_signal_processing), [radio-frequency integrated circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RFIC) (RFIC), and [application-specific integrated circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Application-specific_integrated_circuit) (ASIC) development for the satellite program.[^cite_note-31]

By October 2016, the satellite division was focusing on the significant business challenge of achieving a sufficiently low-cost design for the user equipment. SpaceX President [Gwynne Shotwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwynne_Shotwell) said at the time that the project remained in the "design phase as the company seeks to tackle issues related to user-terminal cost".[^cite_note-sn20161005-32]

### Start of development phase (2016–2019)

In November 2016, SpaceX applied to the FCC for a license to operate a "non-[geostationary orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geostationary_orbit) ([NGSO](45207.md)) satellite system in the [fixed-satellite service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fixed-satellite_service) using the [Ku-](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku_band) and [Ka-](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ka_band) frequency bands".[^cite_note-33] In September 2017, the FCC granted a license that required half of the constellation to be in orbit within six years and that the full system would be operating within nine years from the date of the license.[^cite_note-34]

SpaceX filed documents in late 2017 with the FCC to clarify their [space debris](266344.md) mitigation plan, under which the company was to:

> implement an operations plan for the orderly [de-orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_entry) of satellites nearing the end of their useful lives (roughly five to seven years) at a rate far faster than is required under international standards. \[Satellites] will de-orbit by propulsively moving to a disposal orbit from which they will re-enter the Earth's atmosphere within approximately one year after completion of their mission.[^cite_note-ars20171004-35]



Falcon 9 lifts off from [Cape Canaveral Air Force Station](306778.md) (CCAFS), Florida, delivering 60 Starlink satellites to orbit on November 11, 2019.

In March 2018, the FCC granted SpaceX approval for the initial 4,425 satellites, with some conditions. SpaceX would need to obtain a separate approval from the United Nations' [International Telecommunication Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Telecommunication_Union) (ITU).[^cite_note-fcc20180329-36][^cite_note-ars20180330-37] The FCC supported a [NASA](18426568.md) request to ask SpaceX to achieve an even higher level of de-orbiting reliability than the standard that NASA had previously used for itself: reliably de-orbiting 90% of the satellites after their missions are complete.[^cite_note-38]

In January 2017, SpaceX expected annual revenue from Starlink to reach \$12 billion by 2022 and exceed \$30 billion by 2025. In May 2018, SpaceX expected the total cost of development and buildout of the constellation to approach \$10 billion (equivalent to \$1.23×10<sup>10</sup> in 2024).[^cite_note-nsf20180517-7]

In mid-2018, SpaceX reorganized the satellite development division in Redmond and terminated several members of senior management.[^cite_note-gw20181031-29]

### First launches (2019–2020)

&#x20;A batch of small satellites attached to the rocket with the Earth in the background

The first batch of 60 Starlink satellites stacked together before deployment on May 24, 2019

After launching two test satellites in February 2018, the first batch of 60 operational Starlink satellites was launched on May 24, 2019.[^cite_note-spacex20190515-39]

By late 2019, SpaceX was transitioning their satellite efforts from [research and development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_and_development) to manufacturing, with the planned first launch of a large group of satellites to orbit, and the clear need to achieve an average launch rate of "44 high-performance, low-cost spacecraft built and launched every month for the next 60 months" to get the 2,200 satellites launched to support their FCC spectrum allocation license assignment.[^cite_note-trati20190408-40] SpaceX said they would meet the deadline of having half the constellation "in orbit within six years of authorization... and the full system in nine years".[^cite_note-sn20190426-41]

By July 2020, Starlink's limited [beta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_\(software_development\)) internet service was opened to invitees from the public. Invitees had to sign [non-disclosure agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-disclosure_agreement), and were only charged \$2 per month to test out billing services.[^cite_note-42] In October 2020 a wider public beta was launched, where beta testers were charged the full monthly cost and could speak freely about their experience. Starlink beta testers reported speeds over 150 [Mbit/s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data-rate_units), above the range announced for the public beta test.[^cite_note-43]

### Commercial service (2021–present)



Early Starlink user terminal with dish in early 2021

Pre-orders were first opened to the public in the United States and Canada in early 2021.[^cite_note-44]

The FCC had earlier awarded SpaceX with \$885.5 million worth of federal subsidies from the [Rural Digital Opportunity Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rural_Internet#Innovation_and_solutions) (RDOF) to support rural broadband customers in 35 U.S. states through Starlink,[^cite_note-45] but the subsidies were revoked in August 2022, with the FCC stating that Starlink "failed to demonstrate" its ability to deliver the promised service.[^cite_note-46] SpaceX later appealed the decision saying they met or surpassed all RDOF deployment requirements that existed during bidding and that the FCC created "new standards that no bidder could meet today".[^cite_note-47] In December 2023, the FCC formally denied SpaceX's appeal since "Starlink had not shown that it was reasonably capable of fulfilling RDOF's requirements to deploy a network of the scope, scale, and size" required to win the subsidy.[^cite_note-fccdenial-48]

In March 2021, SpaceX submitted an application to the FCC for mobile variations of their terminal designed for vehicles, vessels and aircraft,[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50] and in June the company applied to the FCC to use mobile Starlink transceivers on launch vehicles flying to Earth orbit, after having tested high-altitude low-velocity mobile use on a rocket prototype in May 2021.[^cite_note-tratii20210701-51] The FCC approved the application in June 2022.[^cite_note-sn20220830-52] Starlink had an annual loss in 2021.[^cite_note-53]

In 2022, SpaceX announced the [Starlink Business](#Services) service tier, a higher-performance version of the service. It provides a larger high-performance antenna and listed speeds of between 150 and 500 Mbit/s with a cost of \$2500 for the antenna and a \$500 monthly service fee.[^cite_note-verge202202-premium-54] The service includes 24/7, prioritized support.[^cite_note-verge202202-premium-54] Deliveries were advertised to begin in the second quarter of 2022.[^cite_note-55] Starlink terminal production was delayed by the [2020–2023 global chip shortage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%932023_global_chip_shortage), and only signed up 5,000 subscribers for the last two months of 2021.[^cite_note-chipshortage-56] Revenues from Starlink in 2022 were reportedly \$1.4 billion, accompanied by a net loss.[^cite_note-57] The Starlink business unit had a single cash-flow-positive quarter during 2022.[^cite_note-58]

On December 1, 2022, the FCC issued an approval for SpaceX[^cite_note-59] to launch the initial 7500 satellites for its second-generation (Gen2) constellation, in three low-Earth-orbit [orbital shells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_shell_\(spaceflight\)#Orbital_shell), at 525, 530, and 535 km (326, 329 and 332 miles) [altitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_altitude). Overall, SpaceX had requested approval for as many as 29,988 Gen2 satellites, with approximately 10,000 in the 525–535 km (326–332 mi) altitude shells, plus approximately 20,000 in 340–360 km (210–220 mi) shells and nearly 500 in the 604–614 km (375–382 mi) shells. The FCC noted that this was not a net increase in approved on-orbit satellites for SpaceX, since SpaceX was no longer planning to deploy 7518 [V-band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V_band) satellites at 340 km (210 mi) altitude that had previously been authorized.[^cite_note-sn20221202-60]

In March 2023, the company reported that they were manufacturing six Starlink "v2 mini" satellites per day as well as thousands of user terminals. The v2 mini had Gen2 Starlink satellite features while being assembled in a smaller [form factor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Form_factor_\(design\)) than the larger Gen2 satellites. The Gen2 satellites required the 9 meters (30 feet) diameter [Starship](59118602.md) in order to launch them.

Starlink posted a \$30.7 million loss in 2023, and its first year of profitability was 2024, with a net profit of \$72.7 million.[^cite_note-\:9-61]

In November 2024, SpaceX proposed a constellation of Starlink satellites around Mars, referred to as "Marslink". The proposed system would be capable of providing more than 4 Mbit/s of bandwidth between Earth and Mars, as well as imaging services.[^cite_note-pcmag20241107-62]

In March 2025, the director of the [United States Department of Commerce](70243.md)'s rural broadband program resigned, citing undue emphasis on Starlink from the [Trump administration](78298108.md).[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65] Also in March, the [Department of Government Efficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Government_Efficiency) (DOGE) installed a Starlink user terminal at the [White House](33057.md) complex which raised [conflict of interest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conflict_of_interest) concerns. In response the White House said that the terminal was donated by Starlink and approved by legal counsel and the [United States Secret Service](58221.md).[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67]

### International concerns

Tensions between Brazil and Elon Musk's business ventures escalated in 2024 as the country's telecom regulator [Anatel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Telecommunications_Agency_\(Brazil\)) threatened to sanction Starlink after Brazil's top court upheld a ban on [X](9988187.md), Musk's social media platform. Brazil's President [Lula](99328.md) supported the decision, citing X's role in allegedly spreading hate and misinformation undermining Brazil's democracy. Brazilian judge [Alexandre de Moraes](53506858.md) froze Starlink's accounts, and Starlink refused to comply with a court order to block domestic access to X until the freeze was lifted, risking its license to operate.[^cite_note-68]

*[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* reported in October 2024 that Musk had been in regular contact with Russian President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) and other high ranking Russian government officials since late 2022, discussing personal topics, business and geopolitical matters. The *Journal* reported that Putin had asked Musk to avoid activating his Starlink satellite system over [Taiwan](25734.md), to appease [Chinese Communist Party general secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) [Xi Jinping](2017814.md). The communications were reported to be a closely held secret in government, given Musk's involvement in promoting the presidential candidacy of [Donald Trump](4848272.md), and his security clearance to access classified government information. One person said no alerts were raised by the U.S. government, noting the dilemma of the government being dependent on Musk's technologies. Musk initially voiced support for Ukraine's defense against Russia's 2022 invasion by donating Starlink terminals, but made later decisions to limit Ukrainian access to Starlink, which coincided with Russian pressure in public and in private.[^cite_note-69] In a November 2024 call with President [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md), Musk said he will continue supporting Ukraine through Starlink.[^cite_note-70]

In 2024, SpaceX asked its numerous Taiwanese suppliers to move production abroad, citing [geopolitical risk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geopolitical_risk) concerns.[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72] This move was questioned by the Taiwanese government and resulted in significant anger from the Taiwanese public, with some pointing out that Starlink was unavailable in Taiwan despite its suppliers providing the underlying technology, and others calling for a boycott of [Tesla](5533631.md) products.[^cite_note-73]



Marslink [walker-delta constellation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_constellation#Walker_Constellation) concept

Starting in July 2024, SpaceX began conducting tests on Starlink in cooperation with the Romanian [Ministry of National Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_National_Defence_\(Romania\)) and National Authority for Communications Administration and Regulation (ANCOM). These tests aim at demonstrating that the Equivalent Power Flux Density (EPFD) limit can be safely increased, thus improving the speed and coverage area of Starlink, without affecting classic, geostationary satellites. The results of these tests will be used to help change a rule set by the [International Telecommunication Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Telecommunication_Union) in the 1990s regarding the limits of non-geostationary satellites.[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75]

In February 2025, Starlink was part of an investigation by [USAID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USAID) into the agency's oversight of Starlink terminals provided to the Ukrainian government, when USAID's Inspector General was fired by President Trump and all employees put on administrative leave. The USAID website was scrubbed of all information related to the Starlink probe.[^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77]

In March 2025, [Musk's involvement in politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_activities_of_Elon_Musk) was protested by a number of Starlink customers in the U.K.[^cite_note-78]

In May 2025, after the Trump administration [launched a series of tariffs](79617151.md), the State Department pushed countries to approve American satellite companies, including Starlink.[^cite_note-79]

## Services

### Satellite internet



Antenna on the ground



Antenna on the roof of a truck



Pole mounted



Starlink user terminal (WiFi router and gateway)

Starlink provides satellite-based internet connectivity to underserved and remote areas, while also competing with terrestrial broadband providers in urban and suburban markets.[^cite_note-80] Starlink also offers mobile connectivity services for uses such as recreational vehicles, maritime vessels, and aviation. Terminals for these markets maintain connectivity while in motion.[^cite_note-Dunphy-81]

Service speeds and latency vary based on network load, location, and service plan. Typical latency is less than that of traditional geostationary satellite internet systems because Starlink's satellites operate in low Earth orbit. Independent testing has shown that performance has varied over time as subscriber growth, satellite deployment, and network upgrades have altered available network capacity.[^cite_note-Ars_on_Ookla_Speed_Study-82]

Because satellite capacity is shared among users within a geographic area, Starlink has implemented network-management policies in some markets. These have included waitlists in high-demand regions, deprioritized service tiers, and data-priority systems intended to manage congestion during periods of heavy network utilization.[^cite_note-Heming-83][^cite_note-Help_Center-84]

Pricing varies by country, service tier, and intended use. SpaceX has periodically adjusted prices in response to regional demand and network capacity, including reducing prices in markets where available capacity exceeded demand.[^cite_note-85] Starlink introduced a monthly 1 TB [data cap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_cap) for all non-business users which was enforced starting in 2023.[^cite_note-Heming-83][^cite_note-ARSTechnica-86] However, Starlink throttling allegedly began after 5TB of usage in September of 2026.[^cite_note-87]

#### Subscribers



Plot of Starlink subscribers



Number of Starlink satellites and user [median](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median) download speed (in [Mbit/s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data-rate_units#Megabit_per_second))

A Starlink subscriber is counted as one active service line. A service line is one internet subscription—usually linked to a specific terminal—and billed under Starlink's standard personal and standard business plans. One service line counts as one subscriber, even if multiple people use it. Likewise, one person or business can have multiple service lines, which are counted separately. It does not include large enterprise or government contracts.

Prior to SpaceX's initial public offering, Starlink used the words "customers", "subscribers", and "people" without providing the definitions.
