### Direct-to-cell service

The direct-to-cell service uses Starlink satellites equipped to connect with standard mobile phones without specialized satellite equipment built-in. Performance varies depending on network load, location, and spectrum availability, with limited capacity compared to terrestrial mobile networks.[^cite_note-ars20220825-102][^cite_note-cnbc-20220825-5]

[T-Mobile US](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-Mobile_US) and SpaceX launched the first commercial implementation in the United States, using T-Mobile midband PCS spectrum to provide coverage in areas without terrestrial mobile service. Initial service supports [SMS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SMS) text messaging, with planned expansion to voice and limited data. The system is designed to work with existing LTE-compatible devices.[^cite_note-tmus-midband-103][^cite_note-2024-01-02_launch-104][^cite_note-auto1-105] On January 8, 2024, SpaceX reported successful SMS testing of the system on T-Mobile's network.[^cite_note-auto1-105]

In April 2023, [Rogers Communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rogers_Communications) announced an agreement with SpaceX to explore satellite-to-phone services in Canada.[^cite_note-Rogers_Signs_on_With_Starlink_satellite-to-phone_service-106] [One NZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_NZ) announced a partnership in the same month to extend mobile coverage across New Zealand, with SMS service expected in 2024 and voice and data in 2025.[^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109] In July 2023, [Optus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optus) announced a similar partnership in Australia.[^cite_note-110] On August 12, 2025, Ukrainian operator [Kyivstar](2254340.md) conducted a test of the service, with a commercial launch planned for 2025.[^cite_note-111]

### Starshield

Main article: [SpaceX Starshield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starshield)

In December 2022, SpaceX announced [Starshield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starshield), a separate Starlink-based service designed for government and military customers.[^cite_note-\:sandraerwin-112][^cite_note-\:starshieldsite-113][^cite_note-\:starshieldSheetz-114] The system is intended for use by the [U.S. Department of Defense](7279897.md) and allied agencies, with options for ownership or leasing of satellites.[^cite_note-\:sandraerwin-112] Starshield satellites are described as supporting encrypted communications and resistance to signal jamming, and are capable of hosting a range of payloads.[^cite_note-\:starshieldSheetz-114][^cite_note-auto-115] Starshield satellites are designed to interconnect with Starlink satellites via optical inter-satellite links.[^cite_note-116]

In September 2023, the U.S. Space Force awarded the first Starshield-related contract under its Proliferated Low Earth Orbit program for satellite communications. The contract is part of a broader procurement effort involving multiple vendors for military LEO services.[^cite_note-\:7-117][^cite_note-118]

## Applications

### Military



Elon Musk and (now retired) four-star general [Terrence J. O'Shaughnessy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrence_J._O'Shaughnessy) meet in April 2019.

SpaceX also designs, builds, and launches customized military satellites based on variants of the Starlink [satellite bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_bus), with the largest publicly known customer being the [Space Development Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Development_Agency) (SDA).

SDA accelerates development of [missile defense](1099196.md) capabilities, primarily via observation platforms, using industry-procured low-cost [low Earth orbit](47568.md) satellite platforms.[^cite_note-119]

In October 2020, SDA awarded SpaceX an initial \$150 million [dual-use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual-use) contract to develop 4 satellites to detect and track ballistic and hypersonic missiles.[^cite_note-120] The first batch of satellites were originally scheduled to launch September 2022 to form part of the Tracking Layer Tranche 0 of the U.S. [Space Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Space_Force)'s [National Defense Space Architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Defense_Space_Architecture) (NDSA), a network of satellites performing various roles including missile tracking.[^cite_note-sda-bet-on-leo-121] The launch schedule slipped multiple times but eventually launched in April 2023.[^cite_note-122][^cite_note-noisyPower-123]

In 2020, SpaceX hired retired four-star general [Terrence J. O'Shaughnessy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrence_J._O'Shaughnessy) who, according to some sources, is associated with Starlink's military satellite development, and according to one source, is listed as a "chief operating officer" at SpaceX.[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-125] While still on active duty, O'Shaughnessy advocated before the [United States Senate Committee on Armed Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_Armed_Services) for a layered capability with lethal follow-on that incorporates [machine learning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine_learning) and [artificial intelligence](1164.md) to gather and act upon sensor data quickly.[^cite_note-126]

SpaceX was not awarded a contract for the larger Tranche 1, with awards going to York Space Systems, Lockheed Martin Space, and Northrop Grumman Space Systems.[^cite_note-127]

#### Military communications

In 2019, tests by the United States [Air Force Research Laboratory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Force_Research_Laboratory) (AFRL) demonstrated a 610 Mbit/s data link through Starlink to a [Beechcraft C-12 Huron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beechcraft_C-12_Huron) aircraft in flight.[^cite_note-spacenews-20191022-128] Additionally, in late 2019, the [United States Air Force](32090.md) successfully tested a connection with Starlink on an [AC-130 Gunship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_AC-130).[^cite_note-defensenews-20200122-129]

In 2020, the [Air Force](32090.md) used Starlink in support of its Advanced Battlefield management system during a live-fire exercise. They demonstrated Starlink connected to a "variety of air and terrestrial assets" including the [Boeing KC-135 Stratotanker](17441.md).[^cite_note-investors-20200923-130]

Expert on battlefield communications Thomas Wellington has argued that Starlink signals, because they use narrow focused beams, are less vulnerable to interference and jamming by the enemy in wartime than satellites flying in higher orbits.[^cite_note-\:11-131]

In May 2022, Chinese military researchers published an article in a peer-reviewed journal describing a strategy for destroying the Starlink constellation if they threaten national security.[^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134] The researchers specifically highlight concerns with reported [Starlink military capabilities](#Military_capabilities). Musk has declared Starlink is meant for peaceful use and has suggested Starlink could enforce peace by taking a strategic initiative.[^cite_note-135] Russian officials including the head of Russia's space agency [Dmitry Rogozin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Rogozin), have warned Elon Musk and criticized Starlink, including warning that Starlink could become a legitimate military target in the future.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-nweek-137]

#### Countermeasures

Main article: [Starlink in the Russo-Ukrainian war § Countermeasures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink_in_the_Russo-Ukrainian_war#Countermeasures)

In response to Ukraine using Starlink in the war with Russia, in particular to guide drones and missiles to targets in Russia, Russia developed a system, named Volna Kupol Garant, to temporarily block Starlink communications. It uses high-powered transmissions that overload the Starlink receiver system, preventing it from receiving signals. Transmissions do not damage Starlink satellites, although they may take several minutes to recover after the jamming transmission is discontinued. The system was announced in August 2026. Sources vary on the size of the jamming zone, one suggesting 20 km<sup>2</sup> (7.7 sq mi) and another a radius of 20 km (12 mi) (1,260 km<sup>2</sup>; 490 sq mi).[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-at-20260805-139]

#### Russo-Ukrainian war



[Vitali Klitschko](555483.md), [mayor of Kyiv](7187856.md), and his brother [Wladimir Klitschko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wladimir_Klitschko) with Starlink terminals shipped to Kyiv during the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md)

Main article: [Starlink in the Russo-Ukrainian war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink_in_the_Russo-Ukrainian_war)

Starlink was activated during the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine), after a request from the Ukrainian government.[^cite_note-Can_Elon_Musk's_Starlink_Keep_Ukraine_Online?_–_Reason_–_Isaac_Reese_reporting-140][^cite_note-elon-musk-b2024184-141] Ukraine's military and government rapidly became dependent on Starlink to maintain Internet access.[^cite_note-\:12-142][^cite_note-143][^cite_note-\:11-131] Starlink is used by Ukraine for communication, such as keeping in touch with the outside world and keeping the energy infrastructure working.[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-\:6-145]

The service is also notably used for warfare. Starlink is used for connecting [combat drones](337755.md), [naval drones](7808557.md), artillery fire coordination systems and attacks on Russian positions.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-\:11-131] SpaceX has expressed reservations about the offensive use of Starlink by Ukraine beyond military communications and restricted Starlink communication technology for military use on weapon systems,[^cite_note-navalnews20230215-147] but has kept most of the service online.[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-bloomberg-149] Its use in attacking Russian targets has been criticized by the Kremlin.[^cite_note-tvpw-150]

Musk has warned that the service was costing \$20 million per month, and a Ukrainian official estimated SpaceX's contributions as over \$100 million.[^cite_note-\:6-145] In June 2023, the [United States Department of Defense](7279897.md) signed a contract with SpaceX to finance Starlink use in Ukraine.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-bloomberg-149]

#### Gaza war

In October 2023 after the [Gaza war](74998519.md) started, users shared the hashtag *#starlinkforgaza* on Elon Musk's social network [X (formerly Twitter)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X_\(formerly_Twitter\)), demanding he activate Starlink in [Gaza](12047.md) after Internet service in the region was lost.[^cite_note-152] Musk answered that Starlink connectivity would be provided for [aid groups in Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_aid_to_Palestinians).[^cite_note-153] At the end of November, Musk said the Starlink service would only be provided for Gaza with the approval of the government of Israel.[^cite_note-154]

#### Mali War and War in the Sahel

According to the [Global Initiative Against Transnational Organized Crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Initiative_Against_Transnational_Organized_Crime), jihadist groups such as the [Jama'at Nasr al-Islam wal-Muslimin](53401088.md) and [Islamic State – West Africa Province](46529902.md) have been using an illicit supply chain to acquire Starlink devices and bypass regulations on their use. The more secure communication of these devices has made it harder for government forces to intercept the plans of armed groups, allowing them to evade law enforcement and sustain illicit economies. A leader of the Union of [Nigeriens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigeriens) for Vigilance and Patriotism, a support group for the Nigerien junta in the [Agadez region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agadez_region), noted:[^cite_note-\:GI-155]

> These days, every suspicious vehicle seems to carry one of these Starlink devices. The armed groups near Emi Lulu, the militias [Haftar](31227894.md) pushed out who are now hiding along the Niger border — they all rely on these devices to stay connected and coordinate their movements.

— February 2025

In response, the governments of Niger and Chad have moved to legalize Starlink in hopes of better regulating the technology by requiring registration of the devices. However, these measures are unlikely to stop the supply chain.[^cite_note-\:GI-155]

Starlink has also been adopted by the Azawadi separatist group, [Azawad Liberation Front](78996050.md) (FLA), as well as the armed forces of Mali. According to an FLA leader in the [Tinzaouaten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tinzaouaten) area, the group uses Starlink to coordinate operations, share intelligence, and communicate its narrative. In the [Battle of Tinzaouaten (2024)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tinzaouaten_\(2024\)), the use of Starlink allowed the FLA to maintain secure communication across its dispersed units and release updates on social media, increasing its visibility to external audiences.[^cite_note-\:GI-155]

#### Iran 2026 war

During [the 2026 Iran conflict](82533582.md), tensions emerged between the United States Department of Defense and SpaceX over the pricing of Starlink services used for military drone operations. SpaceX argued that [LUCAS drones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low-cost_Uncrewed_Combat_Attack_System) were operating under conditions consistent with Starlink's aviation-tier service, while Pentagon officials maintained that the pricing had been intended for aircraft rather than short-duration kamikaze drones.The dispute later resulted in the Pentagon agreeing to higher pricing terms, increasing the communications cost associated with each LUCAS drone. Discussions also expanded to the potential use of Starlink Direct-to-Cell services in Iran during internet shutdowns affecting civilian communications.[^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157]

The Telegraph later reported that Elon Musk accused the Pentagon of violating Starlink's civilian-use agreements by using the network to support armed drone operations during the conflict.[^cite_note-158]

### Criminal

In 2023 that Brazilian [organized criminal groups](22625.md) were making heavy use of Starlink in exploiting remote regions of the [Amazon rainforest](48139.md).[^cite_note-159]

In February 2025, [Wired](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wired_\(magazine\)) and the [BBC](1139893.md) reported that Starlink is a key connectivity option for [scam centers](72348121.md) in Southeast Asia with "criminals running multi-billion-dollar empires across Southeast Asia appear to be widely using the satellite internet network." Wired identified more than one hundred Starlink devices in use at just one center, [KK Park](74691068.md) in [Myanmar](19457.md).[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] SpaceX announced in October 2025, that access to at least 2,500 Starlink devices used in Myanmar scamming centers was cut off.[^cite_note-162]

### Iran

In Iran, [Elon Musk](909036.md) personally announced the activation of Starlink in 2022 after the [Iranian government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_government) blocked the internet to suppress the spread of [anti-government protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahsa_Amini_protests), enabling citizens to regain uncensored access. These cases illustrate the difficulty governments face in controlling unauthorized satellite communications within their borders. The decentralized and autonomous nature of Starlink's operations presents a growing challenge to national sovereignty and cybersecurity enforcement.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166]

Likewise during the [internet blackout](82063825.md) amidst the [2025–2026 Iranian protests](81976180.md), Starlink was activated in Iran.[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] However, by January 11, the Iranian government shut down Starlink internet connectivity for the first time.[^cite_note-169] The jamming and shut down led to varying degrees of success, with users occasionally being able to circumvent the [censorship](12831280.md) amidst the security forces also seizing [satellite dishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_dish) to block any external access.[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171] The [State Department](31975.md) had given SpaceX sanctions exemption to enable Starlink services in Iran, and NGOs including Holistic Resilience and [ASL19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ASL19) had assisted in smuggling terminals into Iran. *[The New York Times](30680.md)* reported that the Iranian authorities had used [GPS spoofing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GPS_spoofing), causing many of the estimated 50,000 Starlink terminals to not operate.[^cite_note-nyt-20260115-172]

### Passenger Wi-Fi on aircraft

Several airlines have retrofitted or announced plans to retrofit aircraft with Starlink terminals to provide [in-flight internet access](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passenger_Wi-Fi_on_airplanes). Airlines have cited its high bandwidth, low latency, and ability to provide connectivity throughout a flight as advantages over previous systems.

On April 25, 2022, [Hawaiian Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Airlines) announced an agreement with Starlink to provide internet service on its aircraft, becoming the first airline to adopt the system.[^cite_note-haw1-173]

In September 2024, [United Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Airlines) announced plans to equip its entire mainline and regional fleet with Starlink connectivity.[^cite_note-abo1-174][^cite_note-ua1-175] The [Federal Aviation Administration](11186.md) approved installation of Starlink equipment on United aircraft in March 2025.[^cite_note-sher1-176]

[Qatar Airways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatar_Airways) began installing Starlink terminals in October 2024. In November 2025, [International Airlines Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Airlines_Group) (IAG) announced it would install Starlink terminals on around 500 aircraft of their fleet ([Aer Lingus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aer_Lingus), [British Airways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Airways), [Iberia](98613.md), [Level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Level_\(airline\)) and [Vueling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vueling)). British Airways was the first IAG airline to fly with Starlink installed in March 2026.[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178] Also in November 2025, [Emirates](142715.md) followed, announcing installations on all 230 aircraft of their active fleet.[^cite_note-179] In December 2025, [Hanjin Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanjin_Group) announced a deal to install Starlink terminals on all aircraft of their fleet, consisting of [Korean Air](315411.md), [Asiana Airlines](331239.md), [Air Busan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Busan), [Air Seoul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Seoul) and [Jin Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jin_Air).[^cite_note-180] In January 2026, [Lufthansa Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lufthansa_Group) announced Starlink would be installed on all around 850 aircraft of their fleet (including Lufthansa, [Austrian Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Airlines), [Brussels Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Airlines), [ITA Airways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITA_Airways),[^cite_note-181] [Swiss International Air Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_International_Air_Lines), [Air Dolomiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Dolomiti), [Edelweiss Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edelweiss_Air), [Eurowings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurowings), and [Discover Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discover_Airlines)).[^cite_note-182]

Other airlines who have announced deals with Starlink include [Zipair Tokyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zipair_Tokyo) in January 2023,[^cite_note-183] [Air France](14998012.md) in September 2024,[^cite_note-184] [WestJet](153851.md) and [Air New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_New_Zealand) in December 2024,[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186] [airBaltic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AirBaltic) in February 2025,[^cite_note-187] [SAS](162868.md) in May 2025,[^cite_note-188] [Virgin Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Atlantic) in July 2025,[^cite_note-189] [Alaska Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Airlines) in August 2025,[^cite_note-190] [Air Canada](145623.md) in September 2025,[^cite_note-191] [FlyDubai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FlyDubai) in November 2025,[^cite_note-192] [Gulf Air](377403.md) in January 2026,[^cite_note-193] [Southwest Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Airlines) in February 2026.[^cite_note-194] [Singapore Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Airlines) and [American Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Airlines) in May 2026,[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196] [Wizz Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wizz_Air)[^cite_note-197] and [El Al](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Al)[^cite_note-198] in June 2026, and [Frontier Airlines](163354.md),[^cite_note-199] [Cebu Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cebu_Pacific),[^cite_note-200] [Volaris](3717400.md),[^cite_note-201] [Copa Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copa_Airlines)[^cite_note-202] [JetSmart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JetSmart)[^cite_note-203] in July 2026 and [VietJet Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VietJet_Air)[^cite_note-204] in September 2026.

### Maritime

Within the shipping industry, Starlink allows location-independent access to telecommunications for sailors.[^cite_note-chosun_2601-205] Cargo ship lines that have installed Starlink internet on their vessels include [Maersk](723005.md),[^cite_note-206] [Hapag-Lloyd](293818.md),[^cite_note-207] [Hyundai Glovis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyundai_Glovis), [Korea Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea_Line?action=edit\&redlink=1), [SK Shipping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SK_Shipping) and [Pan Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_Ocean).[^cite_note-chosun_2601-205]

In August 2022, SpaceX secured its first contract for services in the passenger shipping industry. [Royal Caribbean Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Caribbean_Group) added Starlink internet to its *[Freedom of the Seas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MS_Freedom_of_the_Seas)* liner.[^cite_note-sn20220830-52] By November 2025, Royal Caribbean had installed Starlink on all vessels of its fleet.[^cite_note-208] [Carnival Corporation & plc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnival_Corporation_&_plc) added Starlink in 2024.[^cite_note-209] [Viking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viking_\(cruise_line\)), [Disney Cruise Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Cruise_Line), [Norwegian Cruise Line Holdings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_Cruise_Line_Holdings)[^cite_note-210] added the service in 2023 and [MSC Cruises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MSC_Cruises),[^cite_note-211] [Virgin Voyages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Voyages),[^cite_note-212] and [TUI Cruises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TUI_Cruises)[^cite_note-213] added it in 2024.

## Internet availability and regulatory approval

Starlink availability, June 2025

&#x20;Starlink availability, June 2025&#x20;

---

&#x20; Active

&#x20; Active without approval

&#x20; Approved and pending activation

&#x20; Not covered

The Starlink network has near-global reach, including the polar caps, broadband services are licensed to be provided in 115 countries as of July 2025.

SpaceX formally requested authorization for Canada in June 2020,[^cite_note-cbc20200619-214] the Canadian regulatory authority approved it in November 2020,[^cite_note-ISEDcanada20201106-215] and SpaceX rolled out service two months later, in January 2021.[^cite_note-iphoneCanada20210121-216] By September 2022, Starlink services were on offer in 40 countries,[^cite_note-sx20220910-217] with applications pending regulatory approval in many more.[^cite_note-218]

Starlink satellites passing over the Swiss night sky as seen from [Mürren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%BCrren)

The [ISEDC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innovation,_Science_and_Economic_Development_Canada) allowed Canada to become the first foreign country to approve the service with regulatory approval for the Starlink low Earth orbit satellite constellation on November 6, 2020.[^cite_note-ISEDcanada20201106-215]

In May 2022, Starlink entered the Philippine market, the company's first deployment in Asia, because of a landmark legislative change (RA 11659, Public Services Act) about all-foreign allowance of company ownership in regard to utility entities such as Internet and telco companies. Starlink got provisional permission from the country's collection of regulators and soon began commercial services, aimed at regions with lower Internet connectivity.[^cite_note-219]

In June 2023, a license to offer Internet services in [Zambia](34415.md) was granted to Starlink by the Zambian Government authorities, after the completion of many trial projects throughout the country.[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] In October 2023, Starlink officially went live in Zambia.[^cite_note-223][^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225]

In July 2023, the [Mongolian](19271.md) government issued two licenses to SpaceX to provide Internet access in the country.[^cite_note-226]

In July 2023, it was reported by Bloomberg that attempts to sell the service to Taiwan in 2022 fell through when SpaceX insisted on 100% ownership of the Taiwan subsidiary running Starlink in the country. This went against Taiwanese law that required that Internet service providers (ISP) are at least 51% controlled by local companies, an impracticality when dealing with a globe-spanning ISP.[^cite_note-227]

Japan's major mobile provider, [KDDI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KDDI), announced a partnership with SpaceX to begin offering in 2022 expanded connectivity for its rural mobile customers via 1,200 remote mobile towers.[^cite_note-sn20210922-228]

On April 25, 2022, [Hawaiian Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Airlines) announced an agreement with Starlink to provide free Internet access on its aircraft, becoming the first airline to use Starlink.[^cite_note-haw1-173] By July 2022, Starlink Internet service was available in 36 countries and 41 markets.[^cite_note-sx20220722-LU-MF-BL-229]

In May 2022, it was announced that regulatory approval had been granted for Nigeria, Mozambique,[^cite_note-sn20220527-230] and the Philippines.[^cite_note-sn20220527b-231][^cite_note-232] In the Philippines, commercial availability began on February 22, 2023.[^cite_note-233]

In September 2022, trials began at [McMurdo Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McMurdo_Station) in [Antarctica](18959138.md) and from December 2022 on field missions. Antarctica has no ground stations, so polar-orbiting satellites with optical interlinks are used to connect to ground stations in South America, New Zealand, and Australia.[^cite_note-234][^cite_note-235]

In September 2023, the US-based [United Against Nuclear Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Against_Nuclear_Iran) started donating subscriptions and terminals to Iranians to allow them to circumvent Iran's Internet blackout.[^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237]

In September 2023, it was reported by some Indian news outlets that Starlink would imminently receive its license to operate in [India](14533.md) after Starlink was able to meet all regulatory requirements, but that it would still be required to apply for spectrum allocation in order to provide service.[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239] SpaceX had earlier sold 5000 Starlink preorders in India,[^cite_note-sn20220104-240] and in 2021 had announced that Sanjay Bhargava, who had worked with Musk as part of a team that founded electronic payment firm [PayPal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PayPal), would head the tech billionaire entrepreneur's Starlink satellite broadband venture in India.[^cite_note-241] Three months later, Bhargava resigned "for personal reasons" after the [Indian government](553883.md) ordered SpaceX to halt selling preorders for Starlink service until SpaceX gained [regulatory approval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regulatory_authority) for providing satellite Internet services in the country.[^cite_note-sn20220104-240] In April 2024, it was reported in some Indian news outlets that Starlink had received its "in-principle government approval" and that the approval now "lies at the desk of communications minister Ashwini Vaishnaw".[^cite_note-242]

In November 2023, Starlink received the licenses to operate in [Fiji](10707.md).[^cite_note-243] The service was launched in Fiji in May 2024.[^cite_note-244]

In April 2024, it was reported that the company would begin trial service in [Indonesia](14579.md) in May.[^cite_note-245] Starlink received its license to operate in Indonesia in early May.[^cite_note-246]

In May 2024, Starlink service was made available for pre-order in [Sri Lanka](26750.md), pending regulatory approval from the Telecommunications Regulatory Commission of Sri Lanka[^cite_note-247] (TRCSL).[^cite_note-248] In August 2024, Starlink Lanka (Private) Limited was granted a Telecommunications Service Provider License by TRCSL,[^cite_note-249] allowing it to operate satellite Internet services in the country.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251] However, in March 2025, the government placed the rollout on hold due to concerns over the absence of lawful interception provisions in the license, which were required for national security reasons.[^cite_note-252] By June 2, 2025, the Deputy Minister of Technology confirmed that all regulatory conditions had been fulfilled, including the integration of oversight mechanisms, clearing the way for the launch.[^cite_note-253] Starlink officially launched its services in Sri Lanka on July 2, 2025.[^cite_note-254]

In April 2025, Starlink licence was approved by National Communication Authority in [Somalia](27358.md).[^cite_note-255] In August 2024, Starlink received the licenses to operate in [Yemen](350939.md).[^cite_note-256] Starlink services will soon be implemented through the corporation's sales points distributed across most governorates. These points will provide a full range of services, including device sales, activation, subscription fee payments, and direct technical support.[^cite_note-257] In April 2025, Houthi rebels in Yemen demanded that residents surrender their Starlink devices.[^cite_note-258]

In September 2024, [United Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Airlines) announced it would install Starlink services on the airline's entire fleet, including mainland and regional aircraft, as part of a plan to offer free high-speed Wi-Fi to all passengers.[^cite_note-abo1-174][^cite_note-ua1-175] In March 2025, the FAA issued final approval for United to begin equipping its aircraft with Starlink antennas.[^cite_note-sher1-176]

On October 22, 2024, [Qatar Airways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatar_Airways) launched the first Starlink-equipped [Boeing 777](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_777) flight, flying from [Doha](26214389.md) to [London](17867.md).[^cite_note-259] As of November 2024, [Morocco](19291.md) is set to give regulatory approval to Starlink by 2025.[^cite_note-260]

On March 11 and 12, 2025, Indian telecom companies [Airtel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharti_Airtel) and [Jio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jio_Platforms) have partnered with Starlink to bring satellite Internet to India, aiming to improve connectivity in remote areas.[^cite_note-261] However, the service's rollout is dependent on securing necessary government approvals.[^cite_note-262] These partnerships promise to expand broadband access, especially in underserved regions.[^cite_note-263]

Although Starlink was officially unavailable in [South Africa](17416221.md), it was found in June 2025 that Starlink has been operating unlawfully in South Africa since 2022.[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265] It was reported that South Africans have been able to use Starlink's roaming service for nearly two and a half years as a workaround to the lack of official local support.[^cite_note-266] In June 2025 Starlink began notifiying its users of its Roam Unlimited and Global Roaming plans in South Africa that their service had been suspended.[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268] Earlier in March 2025, Musk claimed that "Starlink can't get a license to operate in South Africa simply because I'm not black". Musk's claim, which likely references the provisions of the South African Electronic Communications Act, 2005, which require telecom licensees to allocate at least 30% equity ownership to historically disadvantaged groups, has been disputed by officials within the South African government.[^cite_note-269]

In February 2026, Starlink received the licences from the [Vietnamese Ministry of Science and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Science_and_Technology_\(Vietnam\)) to operate fixed and mobile satellite networks in [Vietnam](202354.md), through its local unit headquartered in [Hanoi](56667.md).[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272] It is expected that Starlink will deploy four ground gateway stations and provide satellite Internet access to up to 600,000 user terminals.[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274]

In June 2026, India froze final approvals for Starlink to begin commercial operations, with security agencies under the Ministry of Home Affairs withholding clearance amid concerns linked to the reported use of Starlink satellite internet terminals during the ongoing Iran conflict. According to reports, the decision followed allegations that Starlink terminals were used in the Iran–Israel–United States conflict without authorization, raising security concerns in [New Delhi](51585.md) over the control of foreign-operated satellite communication systems during wartime conditions. The suspension came despite Starlink having previously secured a Unified License and GMPCS authorization in 2025.[^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277]

On May 15, 2026, the Uganda Communications Commission (UCC) signed a Memorandum of Understanding and an operational license agreement with Starlink.[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279] The service became commercially available for direct consumer subscriptions in Uganda on September 1, 2026.[^cite_note-280][^cite_note-281]

### Iran

Main articles: [2019 Internet blackout in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Internet_blackout_in_Iran), [2025 Internet blackout in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Internet_blackout_in_Iran), and [2026 Internet blackout in Iran](82063825.md)

In 2022, the U.S. State Department and U.S. Treasury Department updated rules regarding export of technology to Iran, allowing Starlink to be exported to Iran in support of the [Iranian protests against compulsory hijab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_protests_against_compulsory_hijab), which had triggered extensive government censorship.[^cite_note-282] Immediately afterwards, Starlink service was activated in Iran.[^cite_note-\:5-283] In 2023, the Iranian government filed a complaint with the ITU against SpaceX for unauthorized Starlink operation in Iran.[^cite_note-\:10-284] In October 2023 and March 2024, the ITU ruled in favor of Iran, dismissing a SpaceX assertion that it should not be expected to verify the location of every terminal connecting to its satellites.[^cite_note-\:10-284] Iran stated that SpaceX was capable of determining their user terminal locations by citing an October 2022 tweet from Musk saying the number of Starlink terminals operating within Iran was "approaching 100".[^cite_note-\:10-284][^cite_note-285] Despite the illegality of Starlink usage in Iran, the number of Starlink users has skyrocketed via sales of the terminals on the black market. Iranian officials have acknowledged that 30,000 terminals in the country, providing access to some 100,000 users.[^cite_note-286] In a cyber-blockade carried out in response to the protests in early 2026, the Iranian authorities used military-grade jammers that largely disrupted the Starlink signal.[^cite_note-nyt-20260115-172]

## Facilities

Starlink operates a satellite manufacturing facility in [Redmond, Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redmond,_Washington), and user-terminal manufacturing facility in [Bastrop, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bastrop,_Texas).

Starlink's satellite development and manufacturing campus occupies more than 314,000 square feet (29,200 m<sup>2</sup>) across multiple buildings in Redmond, east of [Seattle](11388236.md). The first facility opened in 2015,[^cite_note-287] and the company subsequently expanded into additional buildings within the Redmond Ridge Corporate Center.[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289]

Starlink opened a user-terminal manufacturing facility in Bastrop, Texas, east of [Austin](1998.md), in December 2023. During its first nine months of operation, the one-million-square-foot (93,000 m<sup>2</sup>) facility produced one million user terminals. According to company statements cited by *PCMag*, the facility was projected to become the largest printed circuit board manufacturing site in the United States.[^cite_note-290]

## Technology

### Satellite hardware

|  | This section needs to be **updated**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(August 2026)* |
| --- | --- |



3D rendering of a Starlink satellite

The internet communication satellites were expected to be [smallsats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_satellite), 100 to 500 kg (220 to 1,100 lb) in mass, and were intended to be in [low Earth orbit](47568.md) (LEO) at an altitude of approximately 1,100 km (680 mi), according to early public releases of information in 2015. The first significant deployment of 60 satellites was in May 2019, with each satellite weighing 227 kg (500 lb).[^cite_note-spacex20190515-39] SpaceX decided to place the satellites at a relatively low 550 km (340 mi) due to concerns associated with space debris from failures or low fuel in the [space environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_environment), as well as letting them use fewer satellites than were initially needed.[^cite_note-291] Initial plans forecast in January 2015 were for the [constellation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_constellation) to be made up of approximately 4,000 cross-linked[^cite_note-issconf20150707-292] satellites; more than twice as many operational satellites as were in orbit in January 2015.[^cite_note-announcementEvent-25]

The satellites employ [optical inter-satellite links](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laser_communication_in_space) and [phased array](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phased_array) beam-forming and digital processing technologies in the Ku and Ka microwave bands (super high frequency \[SHF] to extremely high frequency \[EHF]), according to documents filed with the U.S. FCC.[^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294] While specifics of the phased array technologies have been disclosed as part of the frequency application, SpaceX enforced confidentiality regarding details of the optical [inter-satellite links](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-satellite_link).[^cite_note-295] Early satellites were launched without laser links. The inter-satellite laser links were successfully tested in late 2020.[^cite_note-lasertest-296][^cite_note-lasers2-297]

The satellites are [mass-produced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_production), at a much lower cost per unit of capability than previously existing satellites. Musk said, "We're going to try and do for satellites what we've done for rockets."[^cite_note-st20150114-298] "In order to revolutionize space, we have to address both satellites and rockets."[^cite_note-announcementEvent-25] "Smaller satellites are crucial to lowering the cost of space-based Internet and communications".[^cite_note-lat20150116-299]

In February 2015, SpaceX asked the FCC to consider future innovative uses of the Ka-band spectrum before the FCC commits to [5G](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5G) communications regulations that would create [barriers to entry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barriers_to_entry), since SpaceX is a new entrant to the [satellite communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_communications) market. The SpaceX non-geostationary orbit communications satellite constellation will operate in the high-frequency bands above 24 GHz, "where steerable Earth station transmit antennas would have a wider geographic impact, and significantly lower satellite altitudes magnify the impact of aggregate interference from terrestrial transmissions".[^cite_note-300]

Internet traffic via a [geostationary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geostationary_orbit) satellite has a minimum theoretical [round-trip latency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Round-trip_latency) of at least 477 milliseconds (ms; between user and ground gateway), but in practice, current satellites have latencies of 600 ms or more. Starlink satellites are orbiting at 1⁄105 to 1⁄30 of the height of geostationary orbits, and thus offer more practical Earth-to-satellite latencies of around 25 to 35 ms, comparable to existing cable and fiber networks.[^cite_note-301] The system uses a [peer-to-peer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peer-to-peer) protocol claimed to be "simpler than [IPv6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPv6)"; it also incorporates native end-to-end encryption.[^cite_note-302]

Starlink satellites use [Hall-effect thrusters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hall-effect_thruster) with [krypton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krypton) or [argon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argon) gas as the [reaction mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reaction_mass)[^cite_note-spacex20190515-39][^cite_note-303] for orbit raising and [station keeping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_station-keeping).[^cite_note-trati201903-304] Krypton Hall thrusters tend to exhibit significantly higher erosion of the flow channel compared to a similar [electric propulsion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrically_powered_spacecraft_propulsion) system operated with [xenon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenon), but krypton is much more abundant and has a lower market price.[^cite_note-305] SpaceX claims that its 2nd generation thruster using argon has 2.4× the thrust and 1.5× the specific impulse of the krypton fueled thruster.[^cite_note-306]

Second generation V2 Mini satellites have been deployed since February 2023: these craft have two [solar arrays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_array) that are each longer than the single array first generation satellites had and are reported to weigh around 800 kg.[^cite_note-307] Future V2 satellites, which are slated to be delivered to orbit with [Starship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_\(spacecraft\)) spacecraft, are expected to weigh around 2,000 kg and carry solar arrays measuring 20 meters in length. As of August 1, 2026, Starlink's satellite hardware fleet consists of 10,895 active units across first- and second-generation hardware types.[^cite_note-308]

### User terminals



SpaceX board member [Steve Jurvetson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Jurvetson) holding a Starlink user terminal in June 2020



Integrated circuit of Catson-V20, a [SoC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/System_on_a_chip) inside the user terminal

The Starlink system has multiple modes of connectivity including direct-to-cell capability as well as broadband satellite internet service. Direct-to-cell provides connectivity to unmodified cellular phones and is being offered globally in partnership with various national cellular service providers.[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310] Starlink's broadband internet service is accessed via flat user terminals the size of a pizza box, which have [phased array](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phased_array) antennas and track the satellites. The terminals can be mounted anywhere, as long as they can see the sky.[^cite_note-issconf20150707-292] This includes fast-moving objects like trains,[^cite_note-311] and airplanes.[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-promotional-313] Photographs of the customer antennas were first seen on the internet in June 2020, supporting earlier statements by SpaceX CEO Musk that the terminals would look like a "UFO on a stick. Starlink Terminal has motors to self-adjust optimal angle to view sky".[^cite_note-314] The antenna is known internally as "Dishy McFlatface".[^cite_note-315][^cite_note-316]

In October 2020, SpaceX launched a paid-for beta service in the U.S. called "Better Than Nothing Beta", charging \$499 (equivalent to \$594.2 in 2024) for a user terminal, with an expected service of "50 to 150 Mbit/s and latency from 20 to 40 ms over the next several months".[^cite_note-forbes-20201027-317] From January 2021, the paid-for beta service was extended to other continents, starting with the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-teslarati-20210104-318]

A larger, high-performance version of the antenna is available for use with the *[Starlink Business](#Services)* service tier.[^cite_note-verge202202-premium-54]

In September 2020, SpaceX applied for permission to put terminals on 10 of its ships with the expectation of entering the maritime market in the future.[^cite_note-319]

In August 2022, and in response to an open invitation from SpaceX to have the terminal examined by the security community, security specialist Lennert Wouters presented several technical architecture details about the then-current starlink terminals: the main control unit of the dish is a [STMicroelectronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STMicroelectronics) custom designed [SoC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/System_on_a_chip) chip code-named *Catson* which is a quad-core [ARM Cortex-A53](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ARM_Cortex-A53)-based control processor running the [Linux kernel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linux_kernel) and booted using [U-Boot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Das_U-Boot). The main processor uses several other custom chips such as a digital beam former named *Shiraz* and a front-end module named *Pulsarad*. The main control unit controls an array of digital [beamformers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beamforming). Each beamformer controls 16 front-end modules. In addition the terminal has a [GPS receiver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GNSS_software-defined_receiver), [motor controllers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motor_controller), synchronous clock generation and [power over Ethernet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_over_Ethernet) circuits, all manufactured by STMicroelectronics.[^cite_note-320]

In June 2024, a portable user terminal dubbed "Starlink Mini" was announced to be imminently available. The Mini supports 100 Mbit/s of download speed and will fit in a backpack.[^cite_note-321] Initial rollout was in Latin America at a \$200 price point.[^cite_note-322]

### Ground stations

SpaceX has made applications to the FCC for at least 32 [ground stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ground_station) in the United States, and as of July 2020 has approvals for five of them (in five states). Until February 2023, Starlink used the Ka-band to connect with ground stations.[^cite_note-FCC-2020-7-323] With the launch of v2 Mini, frequencies were added in the 71–86 GHz [W band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W_band) (or [E band waveguide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E_band_\(waveguide\))) range.[^cite_note-newscientist.com-324]

A typical ground station has nine 2.86 metres (9.4 feet) antennas in a 400 m<sup>2</sup> (4,300 ft<sup>2</sup>) fenced-in area.[^cite_note-325]

According to their filing, SpaceX's ground stations would also be installed on-site at [Google](1092923.md) data-centers world-wide.[^cite_note-326]

### Satellite revisions

#### MicroSat

MicroSat-1a and MicroSat-1b were originally slated to be launched into 625 km (388 mi) circular orbits at approximately 86.4° inclination, and to include panchromatic video imager cameras to film images of Earth and the satellite.[^cite_note-noaa20151022-327] The two satellites, "MicroSat-1a" and "MicroSat-1b" were meant to be launched together as secondary payloads on one of the [Iridium NEXT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iridium_NEXT) flights, but they were instead used for ground-based tests.[^cite_note-328]

#### Tintin

At the time of the June 2015 announcement, SpaceX had stated plans to launch the first two demonstration satellites in 2016,[^cite_note-nbc20150604-329] but the target date was subsequently moved out to 2018.[^cite_note-cooper_oct17-330] SpaceX began [flight testing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_test) their satellite technologies in 2018[^cite_note-cooper_oct17-330] with the launch of two test satellites. The two identical satellites were called **MicroSat-2a** and **MicroSat-2b**[^cite_note-331] during development but were renamed **Tintin A** and **Tintin B** upon orbital deployment on February 22, 2018. The satellites were launched by a Falcon 9 rocket, and they were piggy-back payloads launching with the [Paz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paz_\(satellite\)) satellite.

Tintin A and B were inserted into a 514 km (319 mi) orbit. Per FCC filings,[^cite_note-Starlink_FCC_TinTin_filing-332] they were intended to raise themselves to a 1,125 km (699 mi) orbit, the operational altitude for Starlink LEO satellites per the earliest regulatory filings, but stayed close to their original orbits. SpaceX announced in November 2018 that they would like to operate an initial shell of about 1600 satellites in the constellation at about 550 km (340 mi) orbital altitude, at an altitude similar to the orbits Tintin A and B stayed in.[^cite_note-fcc20181108a-333]

The satellites orbit in a [circular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circular_orbit) [low Earth orbit](47568.md) at about 500 km (310 mi) altitude[^cite_note-334] in a high-inclination orbit for a planned six to twelve-month duration. The satellites communicate with three testing ground stations in [Washington State](13015878.md) and California for short-term experiments of less than ten minutes duration, roughly daily.[^cite_note-nbc20150604-329][^cite_note-335]

#### v0.9 (test)

The 60 Starlink v0.9 satellites, launched in May 2019, had the following characteristics:[^cite_note-spacex20190515-39]

- Flat-panel design with multiple high-throughput antennas and a single [solar array](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_panels_on_spacecraft)

- Mass: 227 kg (500 lb)

- [Hall-effect thrusters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hall-effect_thruster) using [krypton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krypton) as the [reaction mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reaction_mass), for position adjustment on orbit, altitude maintenance, and deorbit

- [Star tracker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_tracker) navigation system for precision pointing

- Able to use U.S. [Department of Defense](7279897.md)-provided debris data to autonomously avoid collision

  [^cite_note-336]

- Altitude of 550 km (340 mi)

- 95% of "all components of this design will quickly [burn in Earth's atmosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_entry) at the end of each satellite's lifecycle".

#### v1.0 (operational)

The Starlink v1.0 satellites, initially launched in November 2019, have the following additional characteristics:

- 100% of all components of this design will completely [demise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_demisability), or burn up, in Earth's atmosphere at the end of each satellite's life.

  [^cite_note-tasia202104-337]

- K

  a

  -band added

  [^cite_note-gunther-starlink-v1.0-338]

- Mass: 260 kg (570 lb)

- One of them, numbered 1130 and called DarkSat, had its [albedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albedo) reduced using a special coating but the method was abandoned due to thermal issues and [IR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrared) reflectivity.

  [^cite_note-sn20191207-339]

  [^cite_note-340]

- All satellites launched since the ninth launch at August 2020 have visors to block sunlight from reflecting from parts of the satellite to reduce its albedo further.

  [^cite_note-341]

  [^cite_note-342]

  [^cite_note-343]

  [^cite_note-344]

#### v1.5 (operational)

The Starlink v1.5 satellites, initially launched January 24, 2021, have the following additional characteristics:

- Lasers for inter-satellite communication

  [^cite_note-sfn-20210406-345]

- Mass: \~295 kg (650 lb)

- Visors that blocked sunlight were removed from satellites launched from September 2021 onwards.

  [^cite_note-nature-606-7913-346]

#### Starshield (operational)

These are satellite buses with two solar arrays derived from Starlink v1.5 and v2.0 for military use and can host classified government or military payloads.[^cite_note-spacex-starshield-347]

#### v2 (operational)

SpaceX was preparing for the production of Starlink v2 satellites by early 2021.[^cite_note-cnbc20210129-348] According to Musk, Starlink v2 satellites will be "...an [order of magnitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_magnitude) better than Starlink 1" in terms of communications [bandwidth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandwidth_\(computing\)).[^cite_note-349]

SpaceX hoped to begin launching Starlink v2 in 2022. As of May 2022, SpaceX had said publicly that the satellites of second-generation (Gen2) constellation would need to be launched on [Starship](59118602.md), as they are too large to fit inside a [Falcon 9](2647515.md) [fairing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Payload_fairing).[^cite_note-newscientist.com-324] However, in August 2022, SpaceX made formal regulatory filings with the [FCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FCC) that indicated they would build satellites of the second-generation (Gen2) constellation in two different, but technically identical, [form factors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Form_factor_\(design\)): one with the physical structures tailored to launching on Falcon 9, and one tailored for the launching on Starship.[^cite_note-ars20220825-102][^cite_note-SxFCC20220819-350] Starlink v2 is both larger and heavier than Starlink v1 satellites.

Starlink second-generation satellites for launch on Starship were planned, as of 2022, to have the following characteristics:[^cite_note-sfn-212306-351][^cite_note-SxFCC20220819-350]

- Lasers for inter-satellite communication

  [^cite_note-sfn-20212306-352]

- Mass: \~1,250 kg (2,760 lb)

- Length: \~7 m (23 ft)

- Further improvements to reduce its brightness, including the use of a [dielectric mirror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dielectric_mirror) film.

  [^cite_note-starlink-brightness-mitigation-353]

- On 2,016 of the initially licensed 7,500 satellites:

  [^cite_note-\:3-354]

  &#x20;Gen2 Starlink satellites will also include an approximately 25 square meter antenna that would allow T-Mobile subscribers to be able to communicate directly via satellite through their regular [mobile devices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_device).

  [^cite_note-ars20220825-102]

  &#x20;It will be implemented via a German-licensed hosted payload developed together with SpaceX's subsidiary [Swarm Technologies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swarm_Technologies) and [T-Mobile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-Mobile_International_AG).

  [^cite_note-\:3-354]

  &#x20;This hardware is supplemental to the existing [K<sub>u</sub>-band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku_band) and [K<sub>a</sub>-band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ka_band) systems, and inter-satellite laser links, that have been on the first generation satellites launching as of mid-2022.

In October 2022, SpaceX revealed the configuration of early v2s to be launched on Falcon 9.[^cite_note-Gen2-2022-10-04-355] In May 2023, SpaceX introduced two more form factors with direct-to-cellular (DtC) capability.[^cite_note-Gen2-SCS-busses-356]

In August 2025, Starlink tested a "mini laser" to allow connectivity for third party satellites and space stations with the Starlink constellation.[^cite_note-357]

The [bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_bus) design for Starlink satellites has varied considerably since the first units were deployed in space in 2019:

- **Bus F9-1**, 303 kg (668 lb) mass, having roughly the same dimensions and mass as V1.5 satellites. Deployed in Group 5 (see [constellation design](#Second_Generation) section).

  [^cite_note-358]

- **Bus F9-2** (typically called "v2 mini"),

  [^cite_note-ars20220825-102]

  &#x20;up to 800 kg (1,800 lb) mass and measuring 4.1 m (13 ft) by 2.7 m (8 ft 10 in) with a total array of 120 m

  2

  &#x20;(1,300 sq ft). The Solar arrays are 2 in number. It could offer around 3–4 times more usable bandwidth per satellite.

  [^cite_note-359]

  &#x20;They are smaller than Starlink's original ones (and so can be launched on Falcon 9) and have four times the capacity to the ground station to increase speed and capacity. This is due to a more efficient array of antennas and the use of radio frequencies in the [W band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W_band) ([E band waveguide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E_band_\(waveguide\))) range.

  [^cite_note-newscientist.com-324]

  &#x20;They were deployed in Groups 6 and 7 (see [constellation design](#Second_Generation) section).

  [^cite_note-360]

- **Bus F9-3**, F9-2 with direct-to-cellular capability. The bus length was increased to 7.4 m (24 ft). Mass increased to 970 kg (2,140 lb).

  [^cite_note-Gen2-SCS-busses-356]

  &#x20;Deployed in Group 7 (see [constellation design](#Second_Generation) section).

#### v3 (initial deployment)

Starlink v3 satellites are a further enlargement of the design and began operation in 2026. Designed to be launched on Starship, they are too large to be launched on Falcon 9.[^cite_note-spacecom20260928-362] They have a mass of approximately 2,000 kg (4,400 lb), about twice that of the previous v2 version.[^cite_note-spacecom20260928-362]

Each v3 satellite has 1 [Tbit/s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bit_rate) of downlink and 160 Gbit/s of uplink capacity, which is approximately 10 times the downlink capacity and 22 times the uplink capacity of a v2 satellite.[^cite_note-starlink-v3-363] The satellite's phased-array antennas support 2,048 downlink beams and 2,048 uplink beams, compared with 192 downlink and 144 uplink beams on v2.[^cite_note-starlink-v3-363] SpaceX states that a fully-loaded Starship launch of v3 satellites will add approximately 20 times the capacity of a Falcon 9 launch of v2 satellites.[^cite_note-starlink-v3-363] Starship can carry a maximum of about 60 v3 satellites.[^cite_note-spacecom20260928-362]

On September 28, 2026, the 14th flight of Starship deployed 26 v3 satellites into low Earth orbit. It was the first orbital flight of Starship and its first deployment of an operational payload.[^cite_note-spacecom20260928-362]

## Launches

Further information: [List of Starlink launches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starlink_launches)

Between February 2018 and May 2024, SpaceX successfully launched over 6,000 Starlink satellites into orbit, including prototypes and satellites that later failed or were de-orbited before entering operational service.[^cite_note-sn2674441-364] In March 2020, SpaceX reported producing six satellites per day.[^cite_note-365]

The deployment of the first 1,440 satellites was planned in 72 [orbital planes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_plane) of 20 satellites each,[^cite_note-SFN20200903-366] with a requested lower minimum elevation angle of beams to improve reception: 25° rather than the 40° of the other two orbital shells.[^cite_note-fcc20181108a-333]<sup>: 17 </sup> SpaceX launched the first 60 satellites of the constellation in May 2019 into a 550 km (340 mi) orbit and expected up to six launches in 2019 at that time, with 720 satellites (12 × 60) for continuous coverage in 2020.[^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368]

Starlink v3 satellites are launched on [Starship](59118602.md), a SpaceX rocket with a much larger payload capability. The initial announcement in 2019 included plans to launch up to 400 Starlink (version 1.0) satellites at a time.[^cite_note-369] After a suborbital deployment trial on [Starship flight 13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_13) in July 2026[^cite_note-370], the first batch of 26 operational v3 satellites were deployed into orbit on [Starship flight 14](78384681.md) in [September 2026](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_2026).[^cite_note-371]



The Starlink constellation, phase 1, first orbital shell: 72 orbits with 22 each, therefore 1584 satellites at 550 km (340 mi) altitude

### Constellation design and status

In March 2017, SpaceX filed plans with the FCC to field a second orbital shell of more than 7,500 "[V-band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-band) satellites in non-geosynchronous orbits to provide communications services" in an electromagnetic spectrum that has not previously been heavily employed for commercial communications services. Called the "[Very-low Earth orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Very_low_Earth_orbit) (VLEO) constellation",[^cite_note-sn20170302-372] it was to have comprised 7,518 satellites that were to orbit at just 340 km (210 mi) [altitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_altitude),[^cite_note-sn20170917-373] while the smaller, originally planned group of 4,425 satellites would operate in the K<sub>a</sub>- and K<sub>u</sub>-bands and orbit at 1,200 km (750 mi) altitude.[^cite_note-sn20170302-372][^cite_note-sn20170917-373] By 2022, SpaceX had withdrawn plans to field the 7,518-satellite V-band system, superseding it with a more comprehensive design for a second-generation (Gen2) Starlink network.[^cite_note-sn20221202-60]

In November 2018, SpaceX received U.S. regulatory approval to deploy 7,518 V-band broadband satellites, in addition to the 4,425 approved earlier;[^cite_note-374][^cite_note-ars20181115-375] however, the V-band plans were subsequently withdrawn by 2022.[^cite_note-sn20221202-60] At the same time, SpaceX also made new regulatory filings with the U.S. FCC to request the ability to alter its previously granted license in order to operate approximately 1,600 of the 4,425 Ka-/Ku-band satellites approved for operation at 1,150 km (710 mi) in a "new lower shell of the constellation" at only 550 km (340 mi)[^cite_note-376] orbital altitude.[^cite_note-fcc20181108a-333][^cite_note-FCC20181108b-377] These satellites would effectively operate in a third orbital shell, a 550 km (340 mi) orbit, while the higher and lower orbits at approximately 1,200 km (750 mi) and approximately 340 km (210 mi) would be used only later, once a considerably larger deployment of satellites becomes possible in the later years of the deployment process. The FCC approved the request in April 2019, giving approval to place nearly 12,000 [satellites](27683.md) in three orbital shells: initially approximately 1,600 in a 550 km (340 mi) – altitude shell, and subsequently placing approximately 2,800 Ku- and Ka-band spectrum satellites at 1,150 km (710 mi) and approximately 7,500 V-band satellites at 340 km (210 mi).[^cite_note-sn20190426-41] In total, nearly 12,000 satellites were planned to be deployed, with (as of 2019) a possible later extension to 42,000.[^cite_note-spacenews-20191015-378]

In February 2019, a sister company of SpaceX, SpaceX Services Incorporated, filed a request with the FCC to receive a license for the operation of up to a million [fixed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fixed_station) [satellite Earth stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ground_station) that would communicate with its non-geostationary orbit (NGSO) satellite Starlink system.[^cite_note-fcc20190201-379]

In June 2019, SpaceX applied to the FCC for a license to test up to 270 ground terminals – 70 nationwide across the United States and 200 in [Washington state](13015878.md) at SpaceX employee homes[^cite_note-fcc201906-0517-380][^cite_note-fcc_sxapp201906_Q7-0517-381] – and aircraft-borne antenna operation from four distributed United States airfields; as well as five ground-to-ground test locations.[^cite_note-fcc201906-0515-382][^cite_note-fcc_sxapp201906_Q7-0515-383]

On October 15, 2019, the United States FCC submitted filings to the [International Telecommunication Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Telecommunication_Union) (ITU) on SpaceX's behalf to arrange spectrum for 30,000 additional Starlink satellites to supplement the 12,000 Starlink satellites already approved by the FCC.[^cite_note-spacenews-20191015-378] That month, Musk publicly tested the Starlink network by using an Internet connection routed through the network to post a first tweet to social media site [Twitter](9988187.md).[^cite_note-384]

In January 2026 SpaceX announced plans to lower approximately 4,400 satellites from their current 550 km orbit to 480 km over the course of the year, citing improved space safety and reduced orbital decay time for decommissioned satellites.[^cite_note-385]

Early designs had all phase 1 satellites in altitudes of around 1,100–1,300 km (680–810 mi). SpaceX initially requested to lower the first 1584 satellites, and in April 2020 requested to lower all other higher satellite orbits to about 550 km (340 mi).[^cite_note-391][^cite_note-392] In April 2020, SpaceX modified the architecture of the Starlink network.[^cite_note-393] SpaceX submitted an application to the FCC proposing to operate more satellites in lower orbits in the first phase than the FCC previously authorized. The first phase will still include 1,440 satellites in the first shell orbiting at 550 km (340 mi) in planes inclined 53.0°,[^cite_note-SFN20200903-366] with no change to the first shell of the constellation launched largely in 2020.[^cite_note-sn20200421-394] SpaceX also applied in the United States for use of the [E-band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E_band_\(waveguide\)) in their constellation[^cite_note-SpaceX_Starlink_FCC_Application_for_Gen2_Satellites-395] The FCC approved the application in April 2021.[^cite_note-cnbc-20210427-396][^cite_note-397]

On January 24, 2021 [SpaceX](832774.md) released a new group of 10 Starlink satellites, the first Starlink satellites in polar / [SSO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun-synchronous_orbit) orbits. The launch surpassed [ISRO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Space_Research_Organisation)'s record of launching the most satellites in one mission (143), taking to 1,025 the cumulative number of satellites deployed for Starlink to that date.[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-SFN20210124-399]

On February 3, 2022, 49 satellites were launched as Starlink Group 4–7. A [G2-rated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K-index#G-scale) [geomagnetic storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geomagnetic_storm) occurred on February 4, caused the atmosphere to warm and density at the low deployment altitudes to increase. Predictions were that up to 40 of the 49 satellites might be lost due to drag.[^cite_note-400] After the event, 38 satellites reentered the atmosphere by February 12 while the remaining 11 were able to raise their orbits and avoid loss due to the storm.[^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402]

In March 2023, SpaceX submitted an application to add V-band payload to the second generation satellites rather than fly phase 2 V-band satellites as originally planned and authorized.[^cite_note-403] The request is subject to FCC approval.

Starlink February 12, 2023, launch plus 19 hours

Due to delays with Starship development, SpaceX modified the v2 Starlink satellites into a less capable but more compact form factor named "v2 mini", thus enabling these satellites to be launched on Falcon 9.[^cite_note-412] The first set of 21 of these satellites was launched on February 27, 2023. SpaceX committed to reducing debris by keeping the Starlink tension rods, which hold the v2 mini-satellites together during launch, attached to the Falcon 9 second stage. These tension rods were discarded into orbit while launching earlier versions of Starlink satellites.[^cite_note-413] Observations confirm that v2 mini satellites host two solar panels, like the larger v2 satellites.[^cite_note-414]
