Starship began carrying Starlink payloads in test flights with [Flight 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_7) in January 2025. Each of these flights carried up to ten Starlink "simulators" (mass simulators of the satellites) for deployment on a suborbital trajectory, so that they would reenter over the Indian Ocean.[^cite_note-417] Flight 7 failed to reach this objective,[^cite_note-Wattles_2025-418] as did Flights 8 and 9. Successful tests occurred on Flight 10 and [Flight 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_11), with 8 of 8 Starlink simulators deployed each time.[^cite_note-419][^cite_note-420]

[Flight 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_12), on May 22, 2026, was the first flight of the Block 3 Starship and flew a suborbital trajectory. It deployed 22 payloads: 20 Starlink v3 mass simulators and two modified v2 mini satellites equipped with cameras. On [Flight 13](78384679.md), on July 24, 2026, Starship carried 20 production v3 satellites on a suborbital trajectory. It was the first Starship flight to deploy operational Starlink satellites rather than simulators.

On September 28, 2026, [Flight 14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_14) became the first Starship flight to reach orbit, and deployed 26 v3 satellites as Starlink Group 31-1. One Raptor Vacuum engine shut down early during ascent, and Starship compensated by burning its remaining engines for longer. SpaceX said it made contact with all 26 satellites over laser links, ground stations, and user terminals, and that they would perform on-orbit checkouts and raise their orbits before entering service.[^cite_note-spacex-flight14-421]

### Incidents

After the successful launch of Starlink 11-4 the second stage spun out of control and reentered earth's atmosphere over eastern Europe with a fireball visible from Berlin and Sweden as several chunks of space debris crashed into Poland near the city of [Poznań](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pozna%C5%84).[^cite_note-422] No one was hurt in the incident. Several smaller chunks were reported in the village of [Wiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wiry), and it is possible some landed in Ukraine, although officials there did not investigate.[^cite_note-423] The main impact of the incident was that the [Polish Space Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Space_Agency) (POLSA) accidentally sent its report on the incoming space debris to the wrong email address at the [Polish Ministry of Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Ministry_of_Defense), leaving the rest of the government in the dark on its potential hazard and in turn POLSA's president, [Grzegorz Wrochna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grzegorz_Wrochna) would be dismissed from his post.[^cite_note-424]

## Impact on astronomy



Signal pollution in a 333-second exposure image taken from the Blanco four-meter (13') telescope at the [Cerro Tololo Inter-American Observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerro_Tololo_Inter-American_Observatory) in November 2019



Starlink over [Tübingen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%BCbingen), Germany



Starlink 1619 seen by the [Hubble Space Telescope](40203.md)



Timelapse of recently deployed satellites (vertical lines)

[^cite_note-425]

[^cite_note-426]

The planned large number of satellites has been met with criticism from the astronomical community because of concerns over [light pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_pollution).[^cite_note-427][^cite_note-428][^cite_note-429] Astronomers claim that their brightness in both optical and radio wavelengths will severely impact scientific observations. While astronomers can schedule observations to avoid pointing where satellites currently orbit, it is "getting more difficult" as more satellites come online.[^cite_note-430] The [International Astronomical Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Astronomical_Union) (IAU), [National Radio Astronomy Observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Radio_Astronomy_Observatory) (NRAO), and [Square Kilometre Array](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Square_Kilometre_Array) Organization (SKAO) have released official statements expressing concern on the matter. Recent studies have proved that the "unintended electromagnetic radiation" affects radio telescopes creating distortions and excessive noise and the IAU Centre for the Protection of the Dark and Quiet Sky from Satellite Constellation Interference was created to manage these new man-made obstacles to space exploration.[^cite_note-431][^cite_note-432][^cite_note-433][^cite_note-\:8-434]

### Visible optical interference

On November 20, 2019, the four-meter (13') [Blanco telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%ADctor_M._Blanco_Telescope) of the [Cerro Tololo Inter-American Observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerro_Tololo_Inter-American_Observatory) (CTIO) recorded strong signal loss and the appearance of 19 white lines on a [DECam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DECam) shot (right image).[^cite_note-435] This image noise was correlated to the transit of a Starlink satellite train, launched a week earlier.[^cite_note-436]

SpaceX representatives and Musk have claimed that the satellites will have minimal impact, being easily mitigated by pixel masking and [image stacking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Image_stacking).[^cite_note-437] However, professional astronomers have disputed these claims based on initial observation of the Starlink v0.9 satellites on the first launch, shortly after their deployment from the launch vehicle.[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439][^cite_note-440][^cite_note-441] In later statements on Twitter, Musk stated that SpaceX will work on reducing the [albedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albedo) of the satellites and will provide on-demand orientation adjustments for astronomical experiments, if necessary.[^cite_note-442][^cite_note-443] One Starlink satellite (Starlink 1130 / DarkSat) launched with an experimental coating to reduce its albedo. The reduction in g-band magnitude is 0.8 magnitude (55%).[^cite_note-444][^cite_note-445] Despite these measures, astronomers found that the satellites were still too bright, thus making DarkSat essentially a "dead end".[^cite_note-\:2-446]

On April 17, 2020, SpaceX wrote in an FCC filing that it would test new methods of mitigating light pollution, and also provide access to satellite tracking data for astronomers to "better coordinate their observations with our satellites".[^cite_note-SFN20200429-447][^cite_note-448] On April 27, 2020, Musk announced that the company would introduce a new sunshade designed to reduce the brightness of Starlink satellites.[^cite_note-SFN20200429-447] As of 15 October 2020, over 200 Starlink satellites had a sunshade. An October 2020 analysis found them to be only marginally fainter than DarkSat.[^cite_note-449] A January 2021 study pinned the brightness at 31% of the original design.[^cite_note-450]

According to a May 2021 study, "A large number of fast-moving transmitting stations (i.e. satellites) will cause further interference. New analysis methods could mitigate some of these effects, but data loss is inevitable, increasing the time needed for each study and limiting the overall amount of science done".[^cite_note-451]

In February 2022, the [International Astronomical Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Astronomical_Union) (IAU) established a center to help astronomers deal with the adverse effects of satellite constellations such as Starlink. Work will include the development of software tools for astronomers, advancement of national and international policies, community outreach and work with industry on relevant technologies.[^cite_note-452]

In June 2022, the IAU released a website for astronomers to deal with some adverse effects via satellite tracking. This will enable astronomers to be able to track satellites to be able to avoid and time them for minimal impact on current work.[^cite_note-nature-606-7913-346]

The first batch of Generation 2 spacecraft was launched in February 2023. These satellites are referred to as "Mini" because they are smaller than the full-sized Gen 2 spacecraft that will come later.[^cite_note-453] SpaceX uses brightness mitigation for Gen 2 that includes a mirror-like surface which reflects sunlight back into space and they orient the solar panels so that observers on the ground only see the dark sides.[^cite_note-starlink-brightness-mitigation-353] The Minis are fainter than Gen 1 spacecraft despite being four times as large according to an observational study published in June 2023. They are 44% as bright as VisorSats, 24% compared to V1.5 and 19% compared to the original design which had no brightness mitigation.[^cite_note-starlinkmini2brightness-454]<sup>: Table 3 </sup> Minis appear 12 times brighter before they reach the target orbit.[^cite_note-starlinkmini2brightness-454]

### Radio interference

In October 2023, research published in "Astronomy and Astrophysics Letters" had reportedly found that Starlink satellites were "leaking radio signals" finding that at the site of the future [Square Kilometer Array](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Square_Kilometre_Array), radio emissions from Starlink satellites were brighter than any natural source in the sky.[^cite_note-455] The paper concluded that these emissions will be "detrimental to key SKA science goals without future mitigation".[^cite_note-456][^cite_note-\:8-434]

## Increased risk of satellite collision

The large number of satellites employed by Starlink may create the long-term danger of [space debris](266344.md) resulting from placing thousands of satellites in orbit and the risk of causing a [satellite collision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_collision), potentially triggering a cascade phenomenon known as [Kessler syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kessler_syndrome)[^cite_note-457][^cite_note-458] SpaceX has said that most of the satellites are launched at a lower altitude, and failed satellites are expected to deorbit within five years without propulsion.[^cite_note-spacenews_18Sep19-459][^cite_note-gw-460] in near-Earth orbit.

According to SpaceX's semiannual reports filed with the Federal Communications Commission, Starlink satellites performed approximately 50,000 collision-avoidance maneuvers between December 1, 2023, and May 31, 2024, about double the number from the previous six-month period.[^cite_note-461] This represented an average of 14 maneuvers per satellite during the six-month period.[^cite_note-462]

Early in the program, a near-miss occurred when SpaceX did not move a satellite that had a 1 in 1,000 chance of colliding with a European one, ten times higher than the [ESA](10363.md)'s threshold for avoidance maneuvers. SpaceX subsequently fixed an issue with its paging system that had disrupted emails between the ESA and SpaceX. The ESA said it plans to invest in technologies to automate satellite collision avoidance maneuvers.[^cite_note-463][^cite_note-464]

In 2021, Chinese authorities lodged a complaint with the United Nations, saying their space station had performed evasive maneuvers that year to avoid Starlink satellites.[^cite_note-465] In the document, Chinese delegates said that the continuously maneuvering Starlink satellites posed a risk of collision, and two close encounters with the satellites in July and October constituted dangers to the life or health of astronauts aboard the Chinese [Tiangong space station](2481401.md).[^cite_note-466][^cite_note-gw-460]

The destruction of the Russian satellite [Kosmos 1408](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosmos_1408) in November 2021 by an anti-satellite weapon test impacted Starlink operations. According to SpaceX reports, over 1,700 out of 6,873 collision avoidance maneuvers performed by Starlink satellites between December 1, 2021, and May 31, 2022, were to avoid Kosmos 1408 debris.[^cite_note-467]

All these reported issues, plus current plans for the extension of the constellation, motivated a formal letter from the [National Telecommunications and Information Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Telecommunications_and_Information_Administration) (NTIA) on behalf of [NASA](18426568.md) and the US [NSF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Science_Foundation), submitted to the US [FCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Communications_Commission) on February 8, 2022, warning about the potential impact on [low Earth orbit](47568.md), increased collision risk, impact on science missions, rocket launches, International Space Station and radio frequencies.[^cite_note-468]

SpaceX satellites will maneuver if the probability of collision is greater than 10<sup>−6</sup> (1 in 1,000,000 chance of collision), compared to the industry standard of 10<sup>−4</sup> (1 in 10,000 chance of collision).[^cite_note-2022_H1_status_report-469] SpaceX has budgeted sufficient propellant to accommodate approximately 5,000 propulsive maneuvers over the life of a Gen2 satellite, including a budget of approximately 350 collision avoidance maneuvers per satellite over that time period.[^cite_note-Gen2-2022-10-04-355] As of May 2022, the average Starlink satellite had conducted fewer than three collision-avoidance maneuvers over the 6 preceding months.[^cite_note-Gen2-2022-10-04-355]

## Competition and market effects

See also: [Satellite constellation § Communications satellite constellations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_constellation#Communications_satellite_constellations), and [Satellite internet constellation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_internet_constellation)

In addition to the [OneWeb constellation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OneWeb_satellite_constellation), announced nearly concurrently with the SpaceX constellation, a 2015 proposal from [Samsung](46445426.md) outlined a 4,600-satellite constellation orbiting at 1,400 km (870 mi) that could provide a [zettabyte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zettabyte) per month capacity worldwide, an equivalent of 200 gigabytes per month for 5 billion users of Internet data,[^cite_note-470][^cite_note-471] but by 2020, no more public information had been released about the Samsung constellation. [Telesat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telesat) announced a smaller 117 satellite constellation in 2015 with plans to deliver initial service in 2021.[^cite_note-472] [Amazon](90451.md) announced a large broadband internet satellite constellation in April 2019, planning to launch 3,236 satellites in the next decade in what the company calls "[Project Kuiper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Kuiper)", a [satellite constellation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_constellation) that will work in concert[^cite_note-473] with Amazon's previously announced large network of twelve satellite ground station facilities (the "[AWS ground station unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AWS_Ground_Station_unit)") announced in November 2018.[^cite_note-474]

In February 2015, financial analysts questioned established [geosynchronous orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geosynchronous_orbit) [communications satellite](45207.md) fleet operators as to how they intended to respond to the [competitive threat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_competition) of SpaceX and OneWeb LEO communication satellites.[^cite_note-sn201502232-475] In October 2015, SpaceX President [Gwynne Shotwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwynne_Shotwell) indicated that while development continues, the business case for the long-term rollout of an operational satellite network was still in an early phase.[^cite_note-gw201510272-476]

By October 2017, the expectation for large increases in satellite network capacity from emerging lower-altitude broadband constellations caused market players to cancel some planned investments in new [geosynchronous orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geosynchronous_orbit) broadband [communications satellites](45207.md).[^cite_note-477]

SpaceX was challenged regarding Starlink in February 2021 when the [National Rural Electric Cooperative Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rural_Electric_Cooperative_Association) (NRECA), a political interest group representing traditional rural internet service providers, urged the U.S. [Federal Communications Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Communications_Commission) (FCC) to "actively, and aggressively, and thoughtfully vet" the [subsidy](146719.md) applications of SpaceX and other broadband providers. At the time, SpaceX had provisionally won \$886 million for a commitment to provide service to approximately 643,000 locations in 35 states as part of the Rural Digital Opportunity Fund (RDOF).[^cite_note-shields202104-478] The NRECA criticisms included that the funding allocation to Starlink would include service to locations—such as [Harlem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem) and terminals at [Newark Liberty International Airport](248492.md) and [Miami International Airport](128968.md)—that are not rural, and because SpaceX was planning to build the infrastructure and serve any customers who request service with or without the FCC subsidy.[^cite_note-shields202104-478] Additionally, Jim Matheson, chief executive officer of the NRECA voiced concern about technologies that had not yet been proven to meet the high speeds required for the award category. Starlink was specifically criticized for being still in beta testing and for unproven technology.[^cite_note-Khaled202104-479]

While Starlink is deployed worldwide, it has encountered trademark conflicts in some countries such as Mexico[^cite_note-480] and Ukraine.[^cite_note-nvou-20230414-481]

### Similar or competitive systems

Main articles: [Satellite internet constellation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_internet_constellation) and [Satellite constellation § Two-way communication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_constellation#Two-way_communication)

- [Amazon Leo](60415184.md) – a planned 3,276 LEO satellite Internet constellation by an Amazon subsidiary.

  [^cite_note-482]

- [AST SpaceMobile](70955202.md) – a satellite-to-mobile-phone satellite constellation working with large mobile network operators such as Vodafone, AT\&T, Orange, Rakuten, Telestra, Telefónica, etc. with the objective to provide broadband internet coverage to existing unmodified mobile phones.

  [^cite_note-483]

- [Globalstar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalstar) – an operational low Earth orbit (LEO) satellite constellation for satellite phone and low-speed data communications, covering most of the world's landmass.

  [^cite_note-484]

- [Guowang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guowang) – a Chinese low-Earth orbit satellite internet mega constellation, being launched.

  [^cite_note-485]

- [Hughes Network Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hughes_Network_Systems) – a broadband satellite provider providing fixed, cellular backhaul, and airborne antennas.

  [^cite_note-486]

- [Iridium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iridium_satellite_constellation) – an operational constellation of 66 [cross-linked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-satellite_service) satellites in a [polar orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polar_orbit), used to provide satellite phone and low-speed data services over the entire surface of Earth.

  [^cite_note-487]

- [Inmarsat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inmarsat) – a satellite based nautical distress network for transmitting telex, fax, and other text messages since 1979 – typically used in nautical scenarios and disaster scenarios.

  [^cite_note-488]

- [Lynk Global](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynk_Global) – a satellite-to-mobile-phone satellite constellation with the objective to coverage to traditional low-cost mobile devices.

  [^cite_note-489]

- [O3b](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O3b) and [O3b mPOWER](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O3b_mPOWER) – [medium Earth orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medium_Earth_orbit) constellations that provide maritime, aviation and military connectivity, and cellular backhaul; coverage between latitudes 50°N and 50°S.

  [^cite_note-490]

- [OneWeb satellite constellation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OneWeb_satellite_constellation) – a satellite constellation project that began operational deployment of satellites in 2020.

  [^cite_note-cnn20200206-491]

- [Orbcomm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbcomm) – an operational constellation used to provide global asset monitoring and messaging services from its constellation of 29 LEO communications satellites orbiting at 775 km (482 mi).

  [^cite_note-492]

- [Qianfan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qianfan) – a Chinese low-Earth orbit satellite internet megaconstellation, being launched.

  [^cite_note-493]

- [Teledesic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teledesic) – a former (1990s) venture to accomplish broadband satellite internet services

  [^cite_note-494]

- [Viasat, Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viasat,_Inc.) – a broadband satellite provider providing fixed, ground mobile, and airborne antennas.

  [^cite_note-495]

## See also

- [Project Loon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Loon) – Former concept to provide Internet access via balloons in the stratosphere

- [Satellite flare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_flare)

[^cite_note-sn20190426-41]: &#x20;Henry, Caleb (April 26, 2019). ["FCC OKs lower orbit for some Starlink satellites"](https://spacenews.com/fcc-oks-lower-orbit-for-some-starlink-satellites/). SpaceNews. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20201117160100/https://spacenews.com/fcc-oks-lower-orbit-for-some-starlink-satellites/) from the original on November 17, 2020. Retrieved April 28, 2019. lower the orbit of nearly 1,600 of its proposed broadband satellites. The Federal Communications Commission said 26 April 2019 it was correct with SpaceX changing its plans to orbit those satellites at 550 km (340 mi) instead of 1,150 km (710 mi). SpaceX says the adjustment, requested six months ago, will make a safer space environment, since any defunct satellites at the lower altitude would reenter the Earth's atmosphere in five years even without propulsion. The lower orbit also means more distance between Starlink and competing Internet constellations proposed by OneWeb and Telesat. FCC approval allows satellite companies to provide communications services in the United States. The agency granted SpaceX market access in March 2018 for 4,425 satellites using [Ku-band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku_band) and [Ka-band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ka_band) spectrum, and authorized 7,518 V-band satellites in November 2018. SpaceX's modified plans apply to the smaller of the two constellations.

SpaceX seeks experimental authority for two types of testing: (1) a total of 70 user terminals (mixed between the two types of antennas) so that it can test multiple devices at a number of geographically dispersed locations throughout the United States; and (2) up to 200 phased array user terminals to be deployed within the state of Washington at the homes of SpaceX employees for ongoing testing.
